I’m not sure. It’s not something I’ve really tracked. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… organizing is probably one of the things I’m worse at. I don’t mean like my house is a disaster or anything—well, the garage is kind of its own situation, but that’s partly because we kept meaning to put up shelves and then I needed to measure the wall and then the measuring tape disappeared.

But with actual tasks, yeah. My work tickets, notes about home repairs, budget stuff I’m supposed to remember—they end up all over the place. Some are in Slack, some on sticky notes, some buried in email, some in my phone reminders. Then I’ll sit down to do something and spend half the time figuring out where I wrote it down or what I was supposed to be doing in the first place. It’s frustrating because I’m not trying to be careless, it just kind of scatters.

Yeah, I do. It’s not always like I’m pacing around the room or something obvious like that. Sometimes I’ll look totally calm, like I’m sitting on the couch or at my desk, but inside it feels like there’s this… pressure building? Like I need to get up, or switch to a different thing, or mess with something in my hands.

It’s weird because it can happen even when nothing is actually wrong. Like I’ll be sitting there and my husband will be watching a show, and I’m technically watching too, but part of me is like, okay, can we do something else now? Or I’ll start picking at a label on a bottle or fiddling with my ring, and then suddenly I’ve missed half of what happened. Not because I’m worried, exactly, more like I feel like I’ll crawl out of my skin if I just sit still too long.

Like during sprint planning at work, or even mortgage calls, which are already kind of stressful and boring in a very specific way—I’ll know I should just sit there and listen, and then suddenly I’m up getting water or checking something in the other room.\n\nIt’s not like I’m trying to be rude. I just sort of… do it, and then realize afterward that I probably looked distracted.\n\nDoctor: When you were a child, did you also have trouble staying seated in situations where you were expected to stay in your seat?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, I don’t have, like, a perfect memory of every classroom, but I do remember in elementary school teachers moving my desk around sometimes, or reminding me to sit back down because I’d popped up for some reason.\n\nAnd I was also the kid who would start talking before I was actually called on, or rush through an assignment even when I knew how to do it. Which is funny because I did fine with the work, mostly, so I don’t think anyone saw it as a huge problem—more like I was just… a lot in the room.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt unusually energetic or wired for several days in a row?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I have days where I feel kind of revved up, especially if there’s a release deadline or I’ve had too much coffee—which I know is not exactly what you’re asking, probably.\n\nSeveral days in a row, though… I don’t think I’ve noticed something clearly like that. My energy is more sort of jumpy and situational, if that makes sense, not like a whole multi-day stretch where I’m unusually wired.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying excessively about a lot of different things?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it excessive. I worry about normal stuff, I guess—work, money things, family, whether I forgot to reply to someone’s email. Actually that email thing reminds me, I have this one thread right now that’s been sitting there and every time I see it I think, “I should really answer that,” and then it becomes this whole little mental tab.\n\nBut, sorry—your question was about worrying about lots of things. I’m not sure. It’s not something I’ve really tracked. I wouldn’t say I’m constantly worrying all day, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often pick at your skin in a way that feels hard to stop?\n\nPatient: Um… not really, I don’t think. I mean, I’ll mess with a hangnail or something if it’s there, especially if I’m sitting at my desk and notice it, but I don’t think of it as hard to stop.\n\nActually my hands get dry sometimes from washing them or just winter air, and then I’ll notice little rough spots—sorry, that’s probably more detail than you need. But no, I haven’t noticed skin picking being a real problem for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble staying focused on a task, even when you want to pay attention?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Not all the time, but enough that it’s annoying. Like I’ll start reviewing a pull request with every intention of actually reading it carefully, and then a few minutes later I realize I’m just staring at the same chunk of code or the same comment and thinking about, I don’t know, five totally unrelated things.\n\nIt happens with paperwork too—like mortgage documents were a nightmare. I’d be trying to pay attention, because obviously those are important, and then I’d catch myself rereading the same paragraph without absorbing it. Then I get frustrated because I do want to focus, it’s not like I don’t care.\n\nDoctor: When you're trying to focus and your mind drifts, is it usually because you're worrying about something?\n\nPatient: Not usually, no. I mean, sometimes I might remember something I need to do, but it’s not mostly worry. Like not necessarily bad things, just… random stuff. Then I have to pull myself back and reread it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble organizing tasks or keeping track of what you need to do?\n\nPatient: Yeah… organizing is probably one of the things I’m worse at. I don’t mean like my house is a disaster or anything—well, the garage is kind of its own situation, but that’s partly because we kept meaning to put up shelves and then I needed to measure the wall and then the measuring tape disappeared.\n\nBut with actual tasks, yeah. My work tickets, notes about home repairs, budget stuff I’m supposed to remember—they end up all over the place. Some are in Slack, some on sticky notes, some buried in email, some in my phone reminders. Then I’ll sit down to do something and spend half the time figuring out where I wrote it down or what I was supposed to be doing in the first place. It’s frustrating because I’m not trying to be careless, it just kind of scatters.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or unable to relax, even when there isn’t anything specific you’re worried about?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It’s not always like I’m pacing around the room or something obvious like that. Like I need to get up, or switch to a different thing, or mess with something in my hands.\n\nIt’s weird because it can happen even when nothing is actually wrong. Like I’ll be sitting there and my husband will be watching a show, and I’m technically watching too, but part of me is like, okay, can we do something else now? Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected. Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world. Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression. Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight. Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy. Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli. Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences. Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger.
